## Page 1


GLOBAL REGULAR SOLUTIONS
OF THE COMPRESSIBLE NAVIER-STOKES EQUATIONS
WITH NONLINEAR DENSITY-DEPENDENT VISCOSITIES
AND LARGE INITIAL DATA OF SPHERICAL SYMMETRY
GUI-QIANG G. CHEN, JIAWEN ZHANG, AND SHENGGUO ZHU
Abstract. For the physically important case in which the viscosity coefficients depend on
the density ρ through a power law (i.e., ρδ with some exponent δ ∈( 1
2, 1)), we establish
the global well-posedness of regular solutions of the compressible Navier-Stokes equations
for barotropic flow with large initial data of spherical symmetry in two and three spatial
dimensions. The initial density considered here is positive everywhere but vanishes in the
far field, ensuring that the resulting solutions satisfy the conservation laws of total mass and
momentum. The most crucial step in our analysis is to obtain a uniform upper bound for the
density, which is challenging due to the combined difficulties of degeneracy near the far-field
vacuum, coordinate singularity at the origin, and nonlinearity of viscosity coefficients. Fur-
thermore, the methodology developed here can also be applied to the corresponding problem
in which the density remains strictly away from the vacuum.

1. Introduction
The motion of viscous and Newtonian barotropic fluids in Rn (n = 2 or 3) is governed by
the following compressible Navier-Stokes system (CNS):

P = Aργ,
where A > 0 is an entropy constant and γ > 1 is the adiabatic exponent. The viscous stress
tensor T is of the form:

Our results hold for all adiabatic exponents γ ∈(1, ∞) and

2, 1) in R2, and for γ ∈(1, 6δ −3) and δ ∈(7 −2

in R3, without restriction on the size of the initial data. Moreover, the viscosity coefficients
(µ(ρ), λ(ρ)) satisfy the Bresch–Desjardins (BD) relation (see [3]):
λ(ρ) = 2(ρµ′(ρ) −µ(ρ)).
(1.8)
In particular, when ¯ρ = 0 in (1.7), our solutions have the conserved total mass and momentum,
and the fluid density keeps positive in Rn but decays to zero in the far-field, which is consistent
with the facts that the total mass and momentum are conserved, and CNS is a model of non-
dilute fluids.
When (µ, λ, κ) are all constants, there is a vast literature on the well-posedness theory
for CNS. In the absence of vacuum, the one-dimensional (1-D) problem has been studied
extensively (see [31, 33, 34, 60] and the references therein), and the local well-posedness of
the multi-dimensional (M-D) classical solutions are known in [46, 51]. However, when vacuum
appears, the approaches used in the references mentioned above do not work directly, owing
to the degeneracy of the time evolution in the momentum equations, which makes it difficult
to study the dynamics of the fluid velocity in the domain where the density vanishes. In
general, a vacuum is required for the far-field under some physical requirements such as finite
total mass and total energy in Rn. By introducing some compatibility conditions, the local
well-posedness of 3-D regular solutions with vacuum was established successfully in Salvi–
Straˇskraba [50], Cho–Choe–Kim [15], and the references therein. On the other hand, it has
been shown that, in the small data regime (near constant density), the M-D solutions remain
globally regular if the initial data are smooth; see [18, 43] and the references therein. For M-D
problems with general data, though many important results on the global existence of weak
solutions have been obtained in [4, 22, 26, 27, 30, 41], the uniqueness problem It is widely
open due to the fairly low regularity of the solutions.
In fact, when (µ, λ, κ) are all constants, no matter for the barotropic flow or the non-
isentropic flow, the global regularity of M-D solutions with large initial data remains open
until now, even when the initial data exhibit some form of symmetry (if the domain considered
contains the origin). The main obstacle lies in obtaining uniform a priori bounds on the
density, both above and below; see Lions [41], Feireisl [22], Sun–Wang–Zhang [54], and the
references therein. For the M-D problem, as far as we know, such kind of bounds can only be
obtained in the region excluding the origin for spherically symmetric flow; see [16, 26, 27, 29]
and the references therein. Recently, for 3-D spherically symmetric flow, Merle–Rapha¨el–
Rodnianski–Szeftel [44] proved that there exists a set of finite-energy smooth initial data with
far-field vacuum for which the corresponding solutions to the barotropic CNS implode in a
finite time T, i.e., for any ε > 0,
lim
t→T ρ(t, 0) = ∞,
lim
t→T sup

|u(t, x)| = ∞.
(1.9)

3, which corresponds to a monatomic gas, was excluded
in [44] due to a triple-point degeneracy in the underlying analysis. This gap was later filled
by Shao–Wang–Wei–Zhang [52] by introducing a novel renormalization for some autonomous
ODEs. Moreover, for 3-D barotropic flow, in [7], Buckmaster–Cao-Labora–G´omez-Serrano
proved the finite-time blow-up of CNS with strictly positive initial density and spherical
symmetry when γ =
7
5 (corresponding to a diatomic gas).
On the other hand, for 3-D
barotropic CNS without symmetry assumption, Cao-Labora–G´omez-Serrano–Shi–Staffilani
[9] constructed some smooth solutions that remain strictly away from vacuum and nevertheless
develop an imploding finite-time singularity in T3 or R3.

GUI-QIANG G. CHEN, JIAWEN ZHANG, AND SHENGGUO ZHU
When vacuum appears, some singular and counterintuitive behaviors of solutions to CNS
with constant viscosity and thermal conductivity coefficients have been observed. In par-
ticular, Hoff–Serre [28] showed that 1-D weak solutions need not depend continuously on
their initial data when the initial density contains an interval of vacuum states, and Duan-
Xin–Zhu [19] proved that the classical solutions with far-field vacuum cannot preserve the
conservation of the momentum in R3. Such pathological phenomena may be traced back to
the unphysical assumption that the viscosity coefficients are constants when modeling viscous
fluids in the presence of vacuum, under which the vacuum exerts an artificial force on the
fluid across the fluid–vacuum interface. Therefore, from a physical standpoint, compressible
viscous flows near vacuum are therefore more appropriately modeled by the degenerate CNS
with (µ(θ), λ(θ), κ(θ)) shown in (1.5) for the nonisentropic flow or (µ(ρ), λ(ρ)) shown in (1.3)
for the barotropic flow, respectively.
In fact, the degenerate CNS (1.1)–(1.4) (with δ > 0 in (1.3)) has received extensive atten-
tion in recent years. Significant progress on the global existence of smooth solutions has been
achieved when the initial density is strictly positive; see [17, 25, 32, 45] for one-dimensional
flows with general initial data, and [55, 58] for two-dimensional flows with small initial data.
Some significant progress has been made on the global existence of smooth solutions when
the initial density is strictly positive; see [17, 25, 32, 45] for 1-D flow with general data, and
[55, 58] for 2-D flow with small data. However, when infx∈Rn ρ0(x) = 0, the momentum
equations become degenerate in both the time evolution and the spatial dissipation:

of the global existence of M-D weak solutions with finite energy; see Bresch–Vasseur–Yu [5],
Li–Xin [37], Vasseur–Yu [57], and the references therein.
Recently, based on some elaborate analysis of the intrinsic degenerate-singular structures of
the degenerate CNS (1.1)–(1.4), the local well-posedness of classical solutions with far-field
vacuum is known in [11, 38, 39, 59] and the references therein. In stark contrast with the
case that the viscosity coefficients are constants, some solid progress have been obtained on
the global regularity of solutions of the degenerate CNS with large initial data of spherical
symmetry. In particular, when δ = 1, by making full use of the linear dependence of the vis-
cosity coefficient on the density, Chen–Zhang–Zhu [13] have established global well-posedness
of spherically symmetric smooth solutions in Rn (n = 2 or 3) for the following two classes of
initial density profiles:
(i) the initial density ρ0(x) is strictly positive in the whole space Rn:
inf
x∈Rn ρ0(x) > 0;
(ii) the initial density remains positive for all x ∈Rn but decays to zero in the far-field:
ρ0(x) > 0

Furthermore, for the case δ = 1, even when the initial density is compactly supported, under
the setting of the vacuum free boundary problem, it is proved in Chen–Zhang–Zhu [14] that

## Page 6

GUI-QIANG G. CHEN, JIAWEN ZHANG, AND SHENGGUO ZHU
where ⋆denotes the coordinates singularity. In fact, for the 1-D case (m = n −1 = 0), the
so-called coordinate singularity vanishes. Hence, the analysis for M-D spherically symmetric
flows is essentially different from that for 1-D flow. Due to the compressibility of fluids, we
encounter the following two major obstacles:
• possible cavitation, i.e., ρ(t, r) →0 for some (t, r) ∈(0, T] × [0, ∞);
• possible implosion, i.e., ρ(t, r) →∞for some (t, r) ∈(0, T] × [0, ∞).
Overcoming these difficulties is highly nontrivial because of three inherent issues:
• the coordinate singularity at the origin, manifested by the singular factor 1
r in equa-
tions (1.13)1–(1.13)2;
• the degeneracies in both the time evolution (⊛) and the spatial dissipation (♢), arising
from the far-field vacuum in (1.7);
• the strong nonlinearity in the spatial dissipation caused by the nonlinear density-
dependent viscosity coefficients.
Thus, new ideas and techniques are required to establish the global well-posedness of spher-
ical symmetric regular solutions of the Cauchy problem (1.1)–(1.4) with (1.6)–(1.7), especially
when far-field vacuum appears. Fortunately, in this paper, based on some elaborate analy-
sis of the intrinsic degenerate-singular structures of (1.1) in the radial coordinates, we prove
the global well-posedness of regular solutions of the degenerate CNS for barotropic flow with
large initial data of spherical symmetry and nonlinear density-dependent viscosities in two and
three spatial dimensions. Our result holds without restrictions on the size of the initial data,
and the solutions we obtained satisfy the conservation laws of total mass and momentum. In
our analysis, the most crucial step is to obtain the uniform upper bound of ρ, which is chal-
lenging due to the combined difficulties of degeneracy near the far-field vacuum, coordinate
singularity at the origin, and nonlinearity of viscosity coefficients. Owing to the nonlinear
dependence of the viscosity coefficients on the density (i.e., δ ∈(0, 1) in (1.3)) as well as the
appearance of far-field vacuum, the dynamics of the fluid velocity is governed by an intrinsic
singular parabolic system. To handle such a singular structure, we introduce well-designed
singular weighted estimates for the fluid velocity.
Besides, in the simpler case where the
density is strictly positive, our method can be applied directly without major modification.
We now outline the organization of the rest of this paper. In §2, the main theorems on the
global well-posedness for spherically symmetric regular solutions of the Cauchy problem (1.1)–
(1.4) with (1.6)–(1.7) are presented. In §3, we introduce the notations, present an enlarged
reformulation of (1.1), and then outline the main strategies of our analysis. In §4–§8, we
provide a detailed proof of the global well-posedness under the far-field vacuum case stated in
§2. On one hand, in §4–§7, we establish the global uniform estimates for the regular solutions
in carefully designed function spaces, which consist of:
(i) The global a priori upper bound for ρ (§4);
(ii) The global L∞(Rn)-estimate for the effective velocity (§5);
(iii) The non-formation of vacuum inside the fluids in finite time (§6);
(iv) The global singular weighted estimates for regular solutions (§7).
On the other hand, based on these uniform estimates, in §8, we obtain the desired global
well-posedness of regular solutions, by using the method of continuity. In §9, we sketch the
proof for the global well-posedness of regular solutions with strictly positive initial density.
Furthermore, we briefly outline the proof for the desired local well-posedness in §10. Finally,
we list some auxiliary lemmas and new Sobolev embedding theorems for spherically symmetric
functions that are used frequently throughout this paper in Appendices A–C.

2. Main Theorems
This section is devoted to stating our main theorems on the global well-posedness of the
spherically symmetric regular solutions of the Cauchy problem (1.1)–(1.4) with (1.6)–(1.7) for
general data in Rn for n = 2, 3. For simplicity, throughout this paper, for any function space
defined on Rn, the following conventions are used for any k ∈N, unless otherwise specified:

We find that in (2.3), the degeneracies both in the time evolution and spatial dissipation have

It is worth pointing out that, when vacuum appears in the far field, the dynamics of the
velocity field u is governed by the quasilinear singular parabolic system (2.3)–(2.4). Compared
with the case δ = 1 studied in [8, 13, 38], some new obstacles for the global well-posedness
theory with large initial data and far field vacuum arise:
(i) The source term on the right-hand side of (2.3) contains some singularity as
∇ρδ−1 = (δ −1)ρδ−1∇log ρ,
whose behavior is more singular than that of ∇log ρ in [8, 13, 38] due to δ −1 < 0
when ρ →0 as |x| →∞;
(ii) The coefficient ρδ−1 in front of the Lam´e operator Lu tends to ∞as ρ →0 in the far
filed instead of equaling to 1 in [8, 13, 38]. Then it is necessary to show that the term
ρδ−1Lu is well defined in some smooth function space.
Therefore, the two quantities
(ργ−1, ∇ρδ−1)
will play significant roles in our analysis on the high-order regularity of the fluid velocity u.
Based on the above observation, we first introduce a proper class of solutions, called regular
solutions, of the Cauchy problem (1.1)–(1.4) with (1.6)–(1.7).
Definition 2.1. The vector function (ρ, u) is called a regular solution of the Cauchy problem
(1.1)–(1.4) with (1.6)–(1.7) in [0, T] × Rn (n = 2 or 3) for T > 0 if (ρ, u) satisfy the following

It will be shown later that the regular solutions defined here satisfy the conservation of M(t)
and P(t). Next, the regularity of ρ shown in Definition 2.1 implies that ρ > 0 in Rn but
decays to zero in the far-field, which is consistent with the facts that M(t) and P(t) are both
conserved and CNS is a model of non-dilute fluids. Thus, the definition of regular solutions
is consistent with the physical background of CNS.
Our main theorem is on the global well-posedness of the spherically symmetric regular
solutions of the Cauchy problem (1.1)–(1.4) with (1.6)–(1.7) for general initial data. The
regularity of this solution ensures uniqueness in both the 2-D and 3-D cases and demonstrates
that it is a classical solution of this problem.

3. Notations, Reformulations, and Main Strategies
In this section, we first present some notations and conventions in §3.1, which are frequently
used throughout this paper. Next, in §3.2, we introduce an enlarged reformulation for the
degenerate CNS (1.1) to deal with the degeneracy caused by the far-field vacuum. In §3.3,
based on such a reformulation, we show the main strategy and new ideas in our analysis.
3.1. Notations. Throughout the rest of this paper, unless otherwise specified, we adopt the
notations in (2.1) and the following ones.

3.3. Main Strategies. We outline the proof for the global well-posedness of the Cauchy
problem (1.1)–(1.4) with (1.6)–(1.8), including the global upper bound of the density, the
L∞(I)-estimate of the effective velocity, the non-formation of vacuum inside the fluids, and
the uniform estimates for the regular solutions. For brevity, we only sketch the proof for the
3-D case; the 2-D case can be treated in a similar way.
3.3.1. Global uniform upper bound of ρ. The first major task is to obtain the global uniform
upper bound of density ρ. Due to the coordinate singularity at the origin, we divide the proof
into two parts: the interior region B1 = {x : |x| < 1} and the exterior region R3\B1.
In the exterior domain, by the Sobolev embedding H1(1, ∞) ,→L∞(1, ∞), the conservation
of total mass, and the BD entropy estimates (see Lemma 4.2), we obtain
∥ρ∥L∞([0,T]×[1,∞)) ≤C0.
(3.12)
In the interior region B1, the analysis is more subtle due to the geometric singularity. To
overcome this difficulty, we introduce the effective velocity v = u + 2a1δ
δ−1 ∇ρδ−1 (its radial

3.3.4. Global a priori estimates of the regular solutions. With the L∞(I)-bounds for (ρ, v)
established, we turn to the enlarged system (7.2) to obtain the global estimates for the regular
solutions. To simplify the calculations, we provide two auxiliary lemmas (see Lemmas 7.1–7.2
in §7), indicating the equivalence between the W k,p(R3)-norms and weighted Hk(R3)-norms
of the gradient and the divergence of a spherically symmetric vector function, which are useful
in establishing the elliptic estimates for u.
Another key step is to obtain the L2([0, T]; L∞(I))-estimate for Dru.
To this end, we
establish an inequality of the form: |rDru|∞≤C(T)(|rut|2 + 1) via equation (7.2)2 and the
Hardy inequality. This allows us to close the first-order temporal estimate for u, thereby
obtaining Dru ∈L2([0, T]; L∞(I)). With this estimate at hand, together with Lemmas 7.1–
7.2, we can systematically establish the remaining global uniform estimates for (ϕ, u, ψ).

6. Non-Formation of Cavitation inside the Fluids in Finite Time
This section is devoted to showing that the cavitation does not form inside the fluids in
finite time and establishing the lower-bound estimate for the density.

8. Global Well-Posedness of Regular Solutions with Far-Field Vacuum
Based on the local well-posedness stated in Theorem 3.2 and the corresponding global
uniform estimates obtained in §4–§7, we are now ready to give the proof for Theorem 2.1. We
divide the proof into three steps.
1.

9. Global Well-Posedness of Regular Solutions with Strictly Positive
Initial Density
This section is devoted to establishing the global well-posedness of spherically symmetric
classical solutions of the Cauchy problem of system (1.1) with general smooth initial data and
strictly positive initial density, i.e., infx∈Rn ρ0(x) > 0 so that ¯ρ > 0 in (1.7). Certainly, in
this case, under the spherical coordinates, the Cauchy problem (1.1)–(1.4) with (1.6)–(1.7) in
[0, T] × Rn for some T > 0 can be written as the following IBVP in [0, T] × I:

9.1. Local Well-Posedness in M-D Coordinates. To establish the local well-posedness
when infx∈Rn ρ0(x) > 0, we consider the following reformulated system in M-D coordinates:

Notice that the positive lower bound of ρ can be obtained via the transport equation (9.2)1
in some short time.
Then, employing the methods used by Matsumura–Nishida [43] and
Sundbye [55], we can establish the corresponding local well-posedness results of the regular
solutions (as defined in Definition 2.2) when infx∈Rn ρ0(x) > 0. We omit the proof here for
brevity and give the statement of this theorem:
Theorem 9.1. Let n = 2 or 3, ¯ρ > 0 in (1.7), and let (3.8) hold. If the initial data (ρ0, u0)(x)
are spherically symmetric and satisfy (2.15), then there exists T∗> 0 such that the Cauchy
problem (1.1)–(1.4) with (1.6)–(1.7) admits a unique regular solution (ρ, u)(t, x) in [0, T∗]×Rn
satisfying (2.7), (2.9), and (2.16) with T replaced by T∗, and
ρtt ∈C([0, T∗]; L2(Rn)) ∩L2([0, T∗]; D1(Rn)).
Moreover, (ρ, u) is spherically symmetric with form (2.8).
9.2. Global Well-Posedness in Spherically Symmetric Coordinates. The proof for
global well-posedness is organized as follows: First, we establish the uniform upper bound of
ρ. Next, we establish the uniform L∞(Rn)-estimate for the effective velocity, and then obtain
the uniform lower bound of ρ. Finally, based on these, we obtain global uniform estimates for
regular solutions and then show the desired global well-posedness of regular solutions.
This part of the proof can be completed by combining the arguments in §4–§8 with the
method presented in [13, Section 10].
For simplicity, we only list the points that require
noticeable modifications in the following three most important parts:
§9.2.1 Global uniform upper bound of the density;
§9.2.2 Global uniform bound of the effective velocity;
§9.2.3 Global uniform lower bound of the density.

10. Local Well-Posedness of Regular Solutions with Far-Field Vacuum
This section is devoted to establishing the local well-posedness of regular solutions of the
Cauchy problem (1.1)–(1.4) with (1.6)–(1.7) when ¯ρ = 0. We first give the proof of Theorem
3.1 and, at the end of this section, we show that this theorem indeed implies Theorem 3.2.
10.1. Linearization. Let T > 0. In order to solve the nonlinear problem (3.2)–(3.6), we
consider the following linearized problem for (ϕϵ,η, uϵ,η, hϵ,η) in [0, T] × Rn (n = 2, 3):

For the sake of clarity, we declare that the functions (ϕ0, u0) shown above in problem (10.1)
are exactly the ones in (3.5).
It follows from the classical theory [21, 36, 42], at least when η and ϵ are positive, that the
following global well-posedness of (10.1) in [0, T] × Rn holds.
Lemma 10.1. Let (3.8) hold, ϵ > 0, and η > 0. If the initial data (ϕ0, u0)(x) are spherically
symmetric and satisfy (3.9)–(3.10), then, for any T > 0, there exists a unique classical solution
(ϕϵ,η, uϵ,η, hϵ,η) in [0, T] × Rn of the Cauchy problem (10.1) such that

Proof. The well-posedness follows from the classical theories for the transport equation and
the parabolic equations when η and ϵ are positive, which can be found in [36, 42]. We only
show that the solution (ϕϵ,η, uϵ,η, hϵ,η, ψϵ,η) is spherically symmetric and takes form (10.4).
For simplicity, in the rest of the proof, we drop the superscript ϵ and η in (ϕϵ,η, uϵ,η, hϵ,η, ψϵ,η),
and let (ϕ, u, h) be the unique solution of (10.1) in [0, T] × Rn and (ψ, h) satisfy (10.2).

adopt the Einstein summation convention that any index that appears exactly twice in a term
is summed over. For any O = (Olk)n×n ∈SO(n), let x = (x1, · · ·, xn)⊤and y = (y1, · · ·, yn)⊤
satisfying y = Ox. Clearly, yl = Olkxk, OkiOkj = δij, and w(t, x) = O⊤w(t, y).
To show that ˆϕ satisfies (10.1)1, by the following direct calculations:
(ˆϕt + w · ∇ˆϕ)(t, x) = (ϕt + OkiOliwl∂ykϕ)(t, y) = (ϕt + w · ∇yϕ)(t, y),

we find that ˆϕ satisfies (10.1)1 indeed. Clearly, repeating the same calculation, we can also
show that ˆh satisfies (10.1)3.

10.6. The Proof for Theorem 3.2. Based on Theorem 3.1, now we are ready to establish
the local well-posedness of the regular solution of the original Cauchy problem (1.1)–(1.4)
with (1.6)–(1.7) shown in Theorem 3.2.
Proof for Theorem 3.2. It follows from the initial assumptions (2.5)–(2.6) and Theorem 3.1
that there exists T∗> 0 such that problem (3.2)–(3.6) has a unique regular solution (ϕ, u, ψ)
satisfying the regularity property (2.7) with T replaced by T∗, which implies
ϕ ∈C1([0, T∗] × Rn),
(u, ∇u) ∈C([0, T∗] × Rn).

Lemma A.7. Let f = f(x) be a spherically symmetric continuous vector function on Rn.
Then f takes the form: f(x) = f(|x|) x
|x| if and only if
Of(x) = f(Ox)

Conversely, if (A.5) holds, we take the 3-D case as an example. Let x0 ∈Rn be any fixed

The following lemma is on the Aubin–Lions lemma.
Lemma A.9. [53] Let X0 ⊂X ⊂X1 be three Banach spaces. Suppose that X0 is compactly
embedded in X, and X is continuously embedded in X1. Then the following statements hold:
(i) If F is bounded in Lp([0, T]; X0) for 1 ≤p < ∞, and Ft is bounded in L1([0, T]; X1),
then F is relatively compact in Lp([0, T]; X);
(ii) If F is bounded in L∞([0, T]; X0), and Ft is bounded in Lp([0, T]; X1) for p > 1, then
F is relatively compact in C([0, T]; X).

depending only on n such that
∥f∥L∞(Rn) ≤C(n)∥∇f∥Ln(Rn).
(C.2)
Remark C.1. Lemmas C.1–C.2 do not hold for general vector functions or scalar functions.
For Lemma C.1, such examples include f = (1, · · ·, 1)⊤or f = 1. This is mainly owing to
the fact that any spherically symmetric constant vector function must vanish.
For Lemma C.2, consider a function f = f(z) defined on [0, ∞) satisfying
f(0) = 0,
f(z) = | log z|
1
3
on (0, e−1],
f(z) = (ez)−2
on (e−1, ∞).
Define g(x)=f(|x|) and h(x) with hi(x) = f(|xi|) (1 ≤i ≤n). It can be checked that (g, h)
admits the weak derivatives (∂jg, ∂jh) ∈Ln(Rn) (1 ≤j ≤n), while (g, h) /∈L∞(Rn).
